People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I’ve been trying to think if something set it off, but there wasn’t one big change or event that I can identify. It just sort of crept up on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I... I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed myself making plans to hurt myself or anything like that. I guess I just feel really low, but I don’t know that I’d say life isn’t worth living.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another episode in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things in a similar way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. The longest time I can clearly remember was about five months. I felt depressed most days, really tired, and just not like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say excessively. I do worry about things, but I haven’t really noticed it being about a lot of different things all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve noticed my skirts are fitting a little looser, and I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. My staff has noticed it too—like in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts together and actually answer. It’s like my words are moving slower than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually high, energetic, or irritable for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yes. Earlier this month I had a few days where I felt really charged up and upbeat, like I was full of ideas and everything made sense. But then I’d also get irritated really quickly, especially if my husband questioned what I was planning.\n\nDoctor: During those few days when you felt charged up and upbeat, did you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I was only sleeping maybe four hours a night during that stretch, but I’d still wake up feeling ready to go—like I should send emails, sketch out event ideas, or reorganize the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: During those days when you felt especially charged up, did you feel unusually confident or important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. At the time I really believed I could fix the whole morale problem at work, and also get our family routines sorted out, if everyone would just listen to my plan and get on board. It felt very clear to me then.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up days, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. My husband noticed it, and a couple of coworkers did too. They said I was talking really fast and kind of jumping in so much that they couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up days, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Not serious problems, exactly. I’m still getting to work and taking care of things at home. I was only sleeping maybe four hours a night during that stretch, but I’d still wake up feeling ready to go—like I should send emails, sketch out event ideas, or reorganize the kitchen.\n\nDoctor: During those days when you felt especially charged up, did you feel unusually confident or important?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. It felt very clear to me then.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up days, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. My husband noticed it, and a couple of coworkers did too. They said I was talking really fast and kind of jumping in so much that they couldn’t really get a word in.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up days, did your behavior cause serious problems at work, at home, or with other people?\n\nPatient: Not serious problems, exactly. I’m still getting to work and taking care of things at home. But I have noticed I’ve been slower making decisions, less dependable with family plans, and I’ve pulled back from the art stuff I usually do to stay connected with people.\n\nDoctor: During those charged-up days, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I might have been more pushy with my ideas, but I can’t think of anything really risky or impulsive that I did.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that charged-up, upbeat period last?\n\nPatient: The whole swing has been going on about a month — starting with that unusually energized, driven stretch and then moving into feeling low.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
